On a stochastic Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman model
Z. Brzeźniak 1,∗ , A. Ndongmo Ngana 1 and T. Tachim Medjo 2

Abstract
. In this paper, we consider a stochastic version of the Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman model in a smooth two- or three-dimensional domain with dynamical boundary conditions. The system describes creeping two-phase flows and is basically a coupling of the Brinkman equation for the velocity field that governs the flow through the porous media coupled with convective Cahn-Hilliard equations for the phase field, both with two independent sources of randomness given by general multiplicative-type Wiener noises in the Cahn-Hilliard equations. The existence of a weak solution, both in the probabilistic and PDEs sense, is proved. Our construction of a solution is based on the classical Faedo-Galerkin approximation, the Yosida approximation and uses a compactness method. Our paper is the first attempt to generalize the paper [ 17 ] to a stochastic setting.

1
1
footnotetext:
Corresponding author. E-mail address: zdzislaw.brzezniak@york.ac.uk (Z. Brzeźniak).

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we are interested in the Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman equations with random perturbation filling a bounded open domain

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d=2
or
d=3
, with a boundary
\Gamma
which is a
C^{2}
-class riemannian submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
More precisely, given a time horizon
T
, we consider the following stochastic system,

\displaystyle-\diver(2\nu(\phi)D\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})+\lambda(\phi)\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}+\nabla p=\mu\nabla\phi\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}\quad(0,T)\times\mathcal{O},

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\diver\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}\quad(0,T)\times\mathcal{O},

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\diver(\phi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})-\diver(M_{\mathcal{O}}(\phi)\nabla\mu)=F_{1}(\phi)\mathrm{d}W\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}\quad(0,T)\times\mathcal{O},

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta\phi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\phi)\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}\quad(0,T)\times\mathcal{O},

(1.1d)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi+\diver_{\Gamma}(\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})-\diver_{\Gamma}(M_{\Gamma}(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta)=F_{2}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}W_{\Gamma}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad(0,T)\times\Gamma,

(1.2a)

\displaystyle\theta=-\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}G^{\prime}(\varphi)+\varepsilon\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\phi\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad(0,T)\times\Gamma,

(1.2b)

\displaystyle K\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\phi=\varphi-\phi\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad(0,T)\times\Gamma,

(1.2c)

together with the following boundary and initial conditions

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{O}}(\phi)\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\mu=\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad(0,T)\times\Gamma,

(1.3a)

\displaystyle[2\nu(\phi)(D\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}+\gamma(\varphi)\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}]_{\tau}=[-\varphi\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta]_{\tau}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad(0,T)\times\Gamma,

(1.3b)

\displaystyle\phi(0)=\phi_{0}\quad\text{in}~\mathcal{O}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}\quad\Gamma\coloneq\partial\mathcal{O}.

(1.4a)

Here we assume that
W
is a cylindrical Wiener process on a separable Hilbert space
U
and
F_{1}(\phi)
, for every suitable
\phi
, is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
U
to
H^{1}(\mathcal{O})
. Moreover,
W_{\Gamma}
is a cylindrical Wiener process on another separable Hilbert space
U_{\Gamma}
, independent of
W
, and
F_{2}(\varphi)
, for every suitable
\varphi
, is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
U_{\Gamma}
to
H^{1}(\Gamma)
. In (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), the unknown
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}=\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}(t,x)
,
p=p(t,x)
,
\phi=\phi(t,x)
, and
\varphi=\varphi(t,x)
represent, respectively, the velocity vector field, the pressure of the fluid, the phase-field in the bulk and the phase-field on the boundary
\Gamma
.

n
is the unit outward normal vector to the boundary
\Gamma
and
D\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}
is the rate of strain tensor defined by

D_{ij}\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}_{i}}{\partial x_{j}}+\frac{\partial\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}\right).

\nu(\phi)
and
\lambda(\phi)
are the kinematic viscosity and the permeability coefficients, respectively, whereas
\mu
and
\theta
are the chemical potentials in the bulk and on the boundary, respectively. The parameters
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon_{\Gamma}
are positive real numbers which are related to the thickness of the diffuse interface in the bulk and on the surface, respectively. These parameters are fixed through the paper. For any vector
\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\coloneq(\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}})\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}
is its normal component, while
\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}_{\tau}\coloneq\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}-\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}
is its tangential component.

The evolution problem (
1.1a
)–(
1.4a
) does not represent a gradient flow of the local free energy functional

E(\phi,\varphi)=\int_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\phi)\right)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}{2}\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}G(\varphi)\right)\mathrm{d}S+\frac{\varepsilon[K]}{2}\int_{\Gamma}(\varphi-\phi)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S,

(1.5)

in the
\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathcal{O})\times H^{-1}(\Gamma)
metric. Indeed, as one can observe, the bulk-surface Cahn-Hilliard system (
1.1c
)–(
1.4a
) is coupled to the velocity equation (
1.1a
), and consequently, the overall system no longer constitutes a pure gradient flow. Nevertheless, the system (
1.1a
)–(
1.4a
) remains thermodynamically consistent with respect to the free energy functional

E
in the sense that the chemical potentials
\mu
and
\theta
are directly linked, through equations (
1.1d
) and (
1.2b
), to the variational derivatives (or subdifferentials) of
E
with respect to the metric space
\mathcal{H}
. Here
[K]=0
if
K=0
and
[K]=1/K
if
K>0
.
However, we only consider the case
K>0
in this paper.

When studying a two-phase fluid mixture, the equation governing the phase field variable
\phi
(which is generally the Cahn-Hilliard equation) is coupled with an equation describing the averaged velocity
u
of the fluid mixture. A classic choice for modelling this dynamic is the Navier-Stokes system, to which a capillary force, commonly known as the Korteweg force, is added. The latter can be represented as
\mu\nabla\phi
, where
\mu
is the chemical potential in the bulk phases. In the context of diffuse-interface models, one of the most commonly used models for describing the motion of two incompressible viscous fluids with matched (constant) densities is the H-model. Initially introduced in
[
34
]
and later rigorously derived in
[
33
]
, this model involves a system of partial differential equations (PDEs) consisting of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equation coupled to a convective Cahn-Hilliard equation. In recent years, several papers have investigated the Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations (both in deterministic and stochastic contexts) from the perspectives of mathematical theory and numerical analysis, see for example
[
3
,
27
,
33
,
21
,
5
,
30
,
31
,
22
,
46
]
and the reference therein.

In general, the H model is used to describe the dynamics of phase transition phenomena for fluids with the same constant density (matched densities). However, another improvement scheme during a seminar work was proposed by the researchers in
[
5
]
, and their model takes into account the variation of density (unmatched densities). This model is usually called the AGG model. Relevant studies on AGG model’s include those by
[
2
,
28
,
4
]
and the reference therein. Note that in the literature, both the AGG model and the model H have mostly been supplemented with the classical no-slip boundary condition for the velocity field and homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions for the phase field
\phi
and the chemical potential
\mu
in the bulk.
Note that the Euler equation
[
8
]
with a class of dynamic boundary conditions have become extremely popular, and remain so, because they sit at the crossroads of mathematics, physics, and engineering. Furthermore, recently, the H and AGG models with a class of dynamic boundary conditions have become extremely popular, since they more accurately describe the dynamics of phase transition phenomena between bulk and surface. Among these models, we have the Allen-Cahn equation
[
16
]
, the Cahn-Hilliard equation
[
39
,
40
]
, the Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman equation
[
10
,
17
]
, and the Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations
[
29
]
, all subjected with dynamic boundary conditions. In particular, in
[
29
]
, the authors proved the existence of global weak solutions both in two and three dimensions as well as the uniqueness of weak solutions for the deterministic Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations with dynamic boundary conditions and in the case of matched densities. We should point out that for the deterministic system (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
), that is in the absence of the noises terms, the authors in
[
17
]
established the existence of a weak solution in the case
K\geq 0
with regular potentials, and the uniqueness of solution in dimension two. Furthermore, they proved the existence of global weak solutions in the case
K\geq 0
even when the potentials are now singular. Let us point out that in comparison to the model derived and studied in
[
29
]
in the case
K=0
, the model investigated in
[
17
]
take into account the following aspect: The phase-fields
\phi
and
\varphi
are not merely coupled through the trace relation
\phi\lvert_{\Gamma}=\varphi
, but rather through a more general Robin-type coupling condition
K\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\phi=\varphi-\phi
on
\Gamma
, where
K\geq 0
(cf.
1.2c
), and this general condition also reduces to the trace relation when
K=0
.

The present paper is a generalization of the results of
[
17
]
under the presence of random forces. More precisely, with the view to capture the randomness component which may affect the phase-field evolutions, we introduce a Wiener type noise in the Cahn-Hilliard equations themselves. This type of approach was first considered in
[
18
]
with the use of Wiener noise in the well-known stochastic version of the Cahn-Hilliard-Cook model, which was then confirmed as the most accurate description of phase separation, as showed in the contribution work
[
43
]
and the references cited therein. In continuation of these fundamental works, many other stochastic models describing the dynamics of incompressible fluids have also been the subject of intensive investigation in the mathematical literature. For such stochastic models, we refer the reader, for instance, to
[
25
,
21
,
22
,
46
]
in the case of regular potentials like
F_{\text{pol}}
(see (
3.10
));
[
23
,
24
,
46
]
in the case of singular potentials like
F_{\log}
; and also to
[
49
,
50
]
for modified models. For analysis of stochastic Navier-Stokes equations in unbounded domains, see
[
13
,
14
]
.

In this paper, we are interested in studying the stochastic Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman system (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
) with smooth potential (i.e. the potentials
F
and
G
in the free energy functional
E
are allowed to be of class
C^{2}
) using a variational approach.
We prove the existence of martingale solutions to the problem (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
) in both two and three dimension in the case
K>0
.
The proofs of the main results rely on a double approximation through a Faedo-Galerkin discretization in spacial space an Yosida regularization on the nonlinearity.
We derive then uniform estimates on the solutions by applying the Itô formula to the energy functional
E(\phi,\varphi)
(see (
1.5
)) and by using the properties of
\nu,\,\lambda,\,M_{\mathcal{O}},\,M_{\Gamma},\,\gamma,\,F_{1},
and
F_{2}
.
Finally, we pass to the limit by a stochastic compactness argument. The main challenges on the mathematical side in the present paper are the following: for the deterministic system, the integration in space of equations (
1.1c
) and (
1.2a
) together with the boundary conditions (
1.2c
) and (
1.3a
) yields the conservation of the phase field in the bulk
\phi
and the phase field on the boundary
\varphi
during the evolution, and this conservation of mass is then crucial to control the spatial mean of the chemical potentials and also to simplify the mathematical analysis of the system. However, in the stochastic case, the presence of noise in the equations (
1.1c
) and (
1.2a
) deteriorates the system’s mass conservation, making it more difficult to control the chemical potentials
\mu
and
\theta
.

In a subsequent paper, by taking advantage of the present analysis, we aim to study (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
) in the case of singular potential (
3.11
). From the mathematical perspective, the approach to proving the existence of a solution in the case
K=0
differs from the deterministic work
[
17
]
due to the presence of a proliferation terms in equations (
1.1c
) and (
1.2a
). However, we believe that the approach we propose in this first part of the work for solving system (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
) in the case
K>0
can also be applied, but because of the length of the paper, we postpone its investigation in a subsequent paper.

Outline of this paper. Section
2
is devoted to recalling some basic notation and abstract framework.
In Section
3
, we introduce some auxiliary operators, the general assumptions, and we formulate our first main result.
Sections
4
and
5
are devoted to the proofs of several auxiliary results, such as the compactness and tightness criteria, which play a crucial role in establishing the main existence theorem for martingale solutions
in the case
K>0
, see Section
6
.
Additional supporting results related to the proofs of our main theorems are provided in the Appendix.

2.
Deterministic setting of the Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman equations

2.1.
Notation and setting

Throughout the paper, we use the symbol
C
to denote any arbitrary positive constant depending only on the data of the problem, whose value may be updated throughout the proofs. When we want to specify the dependence of
C
on specific quantities, we will indicate them through a subscript. We denote by
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}_{i}
the
i
-th component of a vector field
u
.

Next, we introduce the necessary definitions of functional spaces that are frequently used in this work. For any (real) Banach space
X
, its (topological) dual is denoted by
X^{\prime}
and the duality pairing between
X^{\prime}
and
X
by
\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.80699pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.80699pt\hskip-16.11116ptX^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptX}}}{{\hskip 9.80699pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.80699pt\hskip-16.11116ptX^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptX}}}{{\hskip 8.65288pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.7917pt\hskip 14.7917pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.65288pt\hskip-14.7917ptX^{\prime}\hskip 14.7917ptX}}}{{\hskip 8.65288pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.37509pt\hskip 14.37509pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.65288pt\hskip-14.37509ptX^{\prime}\hskip 14.37509ptX}}}
or simply
\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.7917pt\hskip 14.7917pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.7917pt\hskip 14.7917pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.37509pt\hskip 14.37509pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.37509pt\hskip 14.37509pt}}}
if there is no confusion. Furthermore, we write
(\cdot,\cdot)_{X}
to denote the corresponding inner product.
Given
p\geq 1
, we denote by
L_{w}^{2}(0,T;X)
the Banach space
L^{2}(0,T;X)
endowed with weak topology. In particular,
v_{n}\to v
in
L_{w}^{p}(0,T;X)
if and only if for all
\varphi\in L^{q}(0,T;X^{\prime})
,
p^{\prime}
being the conjugate of
p
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.80699pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\varphi(s),v_{n}(s)-v(s)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-79.7928pt\hskip 79.7928pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.80699pt\hskip-79.7928ptX^{\prime}\hskip 79.7928ptX}}}{{\hskip 9.80699pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\varphi(s),v_{n}(s)-v(s)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-79.7928pt\hskip 79.7928pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.80699pt\hskip-79.7928ptX^{\prime}\hskip 79.7928ptX}}}{{\hskip 8.65288pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\varphi(s),v_{n}(s)-v(s)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.47534pt\hskip 61.47534pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.65288pt\hskip-61.47534ptX^{\prime}\hskip 61.47534ptX}}}{{\hskip 8.65288pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\varphi(s),v_{n}(s)-v(s)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-55.53055pt\hskip 55.53055pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.65288pt\hskip-55.53055ptX^{\prime}\hskip 55.53055ptX}}}\,\mathrm{d}s=0.

For two separable Hilbert spaces
\mathbb{K}
and
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
will denote the space of all Hilbert-Schmidt operators
\mathbb{A}:\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{X}
with norm
\|\mathbb{A}\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})}
, i.e.

\|\mathbb{A}\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})}^{2}\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{\text{dim}\textsection\,\mathbb{K}}\|Ae_{i}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{2},

(2.1)

where
(e_{i})_{i\geq 1}
is an ONB of
\mathbb{K}
. Note that any operator in
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
is compact and
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
is a separable Hilbert space endowed with a scalar product

(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})}=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{dim}\textsection\,\mathbb{K}}(\mathbb{A}e_{k},\mathbb{B}e_{k})_{\mathbb{X}},\;\mathbb{A},\,\mathbb{B}\in\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X}).

The Borel
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{B}(\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X}))
is the
\sigma
-field generated by
\mbox{top}(\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X}))
, i.e. the topology on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
which is induced by the norm (
2.1
) on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
I
be a subset of
[0,\infty)
. The functions spaces
L^{p}(I;X)
consist of all Bochner measurable
p
-measurable functions defined on
I
with values in
X
. We introduce the space
W^{1,p}(I;X)
to consist of all functions
u\in L^{p}(I;X)
with the vector-valued distributional derivative
\partial_{t}u\in L^{p}(I;X)
. If
p=2
, we simply write
H^{1}(I;X)=W^{1,2}(I;X)
.

The set of continuous functions
f:I\to X
is denoted by
C(I;X)
.

Given the Banach space
X
,
C_{w}([0,T];X)
is the space of weakly continuous
f:[0,T]\to X
. More precisely,

\displaystyle C_{w}([0,T];X)\coloneq\{f:

\displaystyle[0,T]\to X;\;\;\mbox{ for all}\;\;\varphi\in X^{\prime},

\displaystyle[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\langle f(t),\varphi\rangle\in\mathbb{R}\mbox{ is continuous}\}.

C_{w}([0,T];X)
is endowed with the locally convex topology induced by the family
\mathscr{P}
of semi-norms given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mathscr{P}_{\varphi}:\;\varphi\in X^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{\varphi}(f)

\displaystyle\coloneq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\lvert\langle f(t),\varphi\rangle\rvert,\;\;f\in C_{w}([0,T];X).

Assume that
d=2
or
d=3
, and
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open domain. Then, define for every
t\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle Q_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,t)\times\mathcal{O},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneq(0,t)\times\partial\mathcal{O}.

For any
p\in[1,\infty)
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
L^{p}(\mathcal{O})
and
W^{s,p}(\mathcal{O})
the usual Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces of scalar functions, respectively. If
p=2
, we simply write
W^{s,2}(\mathcal{O})\coloneq H^{s}(\mathcal{O})
.

We use the notation
\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathcal{O})
,
\mathbb{W}^{s,p}(\mathcal{O})
, and
\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathcal{O})
, to denote the spaces
L^{p}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
W^{s,p}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
H^{s}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, respectively, with
d=2,3
. The Lebesgue spaces and Sobolev spaces on the boundary
\Gamma
are defined analogously.

We denote by
\lvert\cdot\rvert
and
(\cdot,\cdot)
the norm and the scalar product, respectively, on
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
,
L^{2}(\mathcal{O})
, and
H
, see (
2.2
) for the definition of the space
H
.
We will also denote, for the sake of brevity, by
\lvert\cdot\rvert
the norm on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
.
Next, we denote by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
and
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\Gamma}
the inner product and the norm in
L^{2}(\Gamma)
and
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Gamma)
, respectively.

The space
V
, as defined in point (
2.2
) below, is endowed with the norm and scalar product inherited from the space
\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O})
,

(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}})_{V}\coloneq(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}})+(\nabla\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\nabla\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}),\quad\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\,\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in V,

and the norm induced by the scalar product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{V}
in
V
is then given by

\|\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\|_{V}^{2}=\lvert\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert^{2}+\lvert\nabla\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert^{2},\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in V.

The averages of
u
over
\mathcal{O}
and
v
over
\Gamma
are defined respectively by:

\displaystyle\langle u\rangle_{\mathcal{O}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\lvert\mathcal{O}\rvert}\mathchoice{{\hskip 32.33142pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle u,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.94681pt\hskip 22.94681pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-32.33142pt\hskip-22.94681pt(H^{1}(\mathcal{O}))^{\prime}\hskip 22.94681ptH^{1}(\mathcal{O})}}}{{\hskip 32.33142pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle u,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.94681pt\hskip 22.94681pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-32.33142pt\hskip-22.94681pt(H^{1}(\mathcal{O}))^{\prime}\hskip 22.94681ptH^{1}(\mathcal{O})}}}{{\hskip 28.50378pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle u,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.75589pt\hskip 18.75589pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-28.50378pt\hskip-18.75589pt(H^{1}(\mathcal{O}))^{\prime}\hskip 18.75589ptH^{1}(\mathcal{O})}}}{{\hskip 28.50378pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle u,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.42494pt\hskip 17.42494pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-28.50378pt\hskip-17.42494pt(H^{1}(\mathcal{O}))^{\prime}\hskip 17.42494ptH^{1}(\mathcal{O})}}}&\mbox{ if }u\in(H^{1}(\mathcal{O}))^{\prime},\\
\fint_{\mathcal{O}}u\,\mathrm{d}x&\mbox{ if }u\in L^{1}(\mathcal{O}),\end{cases}

\displaystyle\langle v\rangle_{\Gamma}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\lvert\Gamma\rvert}\mathchoice{{\hskip 31.13696pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle v,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.42824pt\hskip 22.42824pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-31.13696pt\hskip-22.42824pt(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{\prime}\hskip 22.42824ptH^{1}(\Gamma)}}}{{\hskip 31.13696pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle v,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.42824pt\hskip 22.42824pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-31.13696pt\hskip-22.42824pt(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{\prime}\hskip 22.42824ptH^{1}(\Gamma)}}}{{\hskip 27.51764pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle v,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.27902pt\hskip 18.27902pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-27.51764pt\hskip-18.27902pt(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{\prime}\hskip 18.27902ptH^{1}(\Gamma)}}}{{\hskip 27.51764pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle v,1\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.98508pt\hskip 16.98508pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-27.51764pt\hskip-16.98508pt(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{\prime}\hskip 16.98508ptH^{1}(\Gamma)}}}&\mbox{ if }v\in(H^{1}(\Gamma))^{\prime},\\
\fint_{\Gamma}v\,\mathrm{d}S&\mbox{ if }v\in L^{1}(\Gamma).\end{cases}

Here
1
represents the constant function assuming
1
in
\mathcal{O}
and on
\Gamma
, respectively, while
\lvert\mathcal{O}\rvert
and
\lvert\Gamma\rvert
stand for the
d
-dimensional and
d-1
-dimensional Lebesgue measures of
\mathcal{O}
and
\Gamma
, respectively.

If
\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}:\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we put

\displaystyle\lvert\nabla\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert D_{i}\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\lvert D_{i}\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}_{j}\rvert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}^{2},

\displaystyle\lvert D\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})}

\displaystyle\coloneq\sum_{i,j=1}\left\lvert\frac{1}{2}(D_{j}\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}_{i}+D_{i}\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}_{j})\right\rvert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}^{2}.

We set

\mathcal{C}_{0,\sigma}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O})=\{\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d}):~\diver\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}=0\mbox{ in }\mathcal{O}\},

and for the mathematical setting of (
1.1a
)–(
1.4a
) we introduce the Hilbert spaces

\displaystyle H(\diver,\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle=\{\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O});~\diver\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O})\},

(2.2)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\text{the closure of}~\mathcal{C}_{0,\sigma}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O})~\mbox{ in }~\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O})\cap H.

The space
H
can also be characterised as
[
53
, Theorem 1.1/1.4]
:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\{\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in H(\diver,\mathcal{O}):\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}=0\mbox{ on }\Gamma\}.

For every
k\in[0,\infty)
, we introduce the following standard scale of Hilbert spaces

\mathcal{H}^{k}\coloneq H^{k}(\mathcal{O})\times H^{k}(\Gamma),

which are endowed with the standard inner product

((u,f),(v,g))_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}\coloneq(u,v)_{H^{k}(\mathcal{O})}+(f,g)_{H^{k}(\Gamma)}\;\;(u,f),\;(v,g)\in\mathbb{H}^{k}

and the induced norm

\|(u,f)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}^{2}=((u,f),(u,f))_{\mathcal{H}^{k}},\;\;(u,f)\in\mathcal{H}^{k}.

Consequently,
(\mathcal{H}^{k},(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^{k}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}})
is a Hilbert space. Note that for
k=0
,

\mathbb{H}\coloneq L^{2}(\mathcal{O})\times L^{2}(\Gamma)=\mathcal{H}^{0}.

Subsequently, we introduce the product:

((u,f),(v,g))_{\mathbb{H}}\coloneq(u,v)+(f,g)_{\Gamma},\;\;(u,f),\,(v,g)\in\mathbb{H}.

We set
\mathbb{V}=H^{1}(\mathcal{O})\times H^{1}(\Gamma)
and endow the space
\mathbb{V}
with the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathbb{V}}\coloneq(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
and its induced norm. This means that
(\mathbb{V},(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathbb{V}},\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}})
is also a Hilbert space. Since the space
\mathbb{V}
is densely and continuously embedded in
\mathbb{H}
, then by identifying
\mathbb{H}
with its dual space
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
, we have the following Gelfand chain, where each space is densely and compactly embedded into the next one,

\mathbb{V}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}\cong\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}^{\prime}.

For two Banach spaces
X_{i},\,i=1,2
, we understand that
(\prod_{i=1}^{2}X_{i})^{\prime}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{2}X_{i}^{\prime}
.
So,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle Z^{\ast},Z\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.90279pt\hskip 31.90279pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-31.90279pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 31.90279pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle Z^{\ast},Z\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.90279pt\hskip 31.90279pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-31.90279pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 31.90279pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle Z^{\ast},Z\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-26.10007pt\hskip 26.10007pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-26.10007pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 26.10007pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle Z^{\ast},Z\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-24.08354pt\hskip 24.08354pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-24.08354pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 24.08354pt\mathbb{V}}}}=\mathchoice{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle Z_{1}^{\ast},Z_{1}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.38892pt\hskip 36.38892pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-36.38892ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 36.38892ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle Z_{1}^{\ast},Z_{1}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.38892pt\hskip 36.38892pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-36.38892ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 36.38892ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle Z_{1}^{\ast},Z_{1}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-30.0029pt\hskip 30.0029pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-30.0029ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 30.0029ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle Z_{1}^{\ast},Z_{1}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.98637pt\hskip 27.98637pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-27.98637ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 27.98637ptV_{1}}}}+\mathchoice{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle Z_{2}^{\ast},Z_{2}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.38892pt\hskip 36.38892pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-36.38892ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 36.38892ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle Z_{2}^{\ast},Z_{2}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.38892pt\hskip 36.38892pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-36.38892ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 36.38892ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle Z_{2}^{\ast},Z_{2}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-30.0029pt\hskip 30.0029pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-30.0029ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 30.0029ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle Z_{2}^{\ast},Z_{2}\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.98637pt\hskip 27.98637pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-27.98637ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 27.98637ptV_{\Gamma}}}},\;\;Z\in\mathbb{V},\;Z^{\ast}\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime},

having set, for the sake of brevity,

V_{1}\coloneq H^{1}(\mathcal{O})\mbox{ and }V_{\Gamma}\coloneq H^{1}(\Gamma).

The dual norm on
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
is given by

\|(u,f)\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}\coloneq\sup\{|\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle(u,f),(v,g)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-59.47574pt\hskip 59.47574pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-59.47574pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 59.47574pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle(u,f),(v,g)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-59.47574pt\hskip 59.47574pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-59.47574pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 59.47574pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle(u,f),(v,g)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-45.44438pt\hskip 45.44438pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-45.44438pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 45.44438pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle(u,f),(v,g)\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-40.36667pt\hskip 40.36667pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-40.36667pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 40.36667pt\mathbb{V}}}}\rvert\;:\;(v,g)\in\mathbb{V}\mbox{ with }\|(v,g)\|_{\mathbb{V}}=1\},\;\;(u,f)\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}.

We conclude this subsection by recalling the following Korn inequality, cf.
[
1
,
17
]
, and the bulk-surface Poincaré inequality, see
[
38
, Lemma A.1]
, respectively, and which will be used throughout this work.

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume
\mathcal{O}
is of class
C^{2}
. Then, there exists
C_{\text{KN}}>0
such that

\lvert\nabla\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert\leq C_{\text{KN}}(\mathcal{O})(\lvert D\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert+\lvert\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\rvert_{\Gamma}),\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O}).

(2.3)

The following lemma is a direct consequence of the bulk surface Poincaré inequality derived in
[
38
, Lemma A.1]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{O}
is of class
C^{2}
. Then, there exists
C_{\text{PC}}>0
depending only on
\mathcal{O}
such that

\left\lvert v-\fint_{\Gamma}v\,\mathrm{d}S\right\rvert\leq C_{\text{PC}}\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}v\rvert_{\Gamma},\;\;v\in H^{1}(\Gamma).

(2.4)

2.2.
Setting of bilinear operators and their properties

Let
\boldsymbol{\Pi}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})\to H
be the orthogonal projection usually called the Helmhotz-Leray projection. It is known, see
[
53
, Remark 1.6]
, that
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
maps continuously the Hilbert space
H^{1}(\mathcal{O})
into itself.

From now on, we denote by
A
the Stokes operator in the case of Navier boundary conditions, which is defined by, see, e.g.,
[
1
, Appendix A]
,

\displaystyle D(\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}})

\displaystyle\coloneq\{\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in H^{2}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap H:\,[2(D\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}})+\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}]_{\tau}=0\mbox{ on }\Gamma\},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}}\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}

\displaystyle\coloneq-\boldsymbol{\Pi}\diver(2D\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}),\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in D(\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}}).

Notice that
A
is a symmetric operator since

(\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}}\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}},\mbox{\boldmath{$\mathrm{w}$}})=2\int_{\mathcal{O}}D\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}:D\mbox{\boldmath{$\mathrm{w}$}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$\mathrm{w}$}}\,\mathrm{d}S=(\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}},\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}}\mbox{\boldmath{$\mathrm{w}$}}),\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}},\,\mbox{\boldmath{$\mathrm{w}$}}\in D(\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}}).

Moreover,
A
is a positive and self-adjoint operator on
H
with compact inverse and
V=(H,D(\mbox{\boldmath{$A$}}))_{\frac{1}{2},2}
.

Now and throughout this paper, we assume that
\Gamma
is a
C^{2}
-class riemannian submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
endowed with the metric induced from

\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Thus, we have the scale
H^{s}(\Gamma)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
of Sobolev spaces, see e.g.
[
51
]
.

We denote by
\Delta_{\Gamma}:L^{2}(\Gamma)\to L^{2}(\Gamma)
the corresponding Laplace-Beltrami operator on
L^{2}(\Gamma)
defined by

\displaystyle D(\Delta_{\Gamma})

\displaystyle\coloneq H^{2}(\Gamma),

(2.6)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma}u

\displaystyle\coloneq\diver_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}u,\;\;u\in D(\Delta_{\Gamma}),

where
\diver_{\Gamma}
denotes the surface divergence,
\nabla_{\Gamma}\coloneq(\partial_{\tau_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{\tau_{d-1}})
the Riemannian gradient on
\Gamma
, and
\partial_{\tau_{i}}
the derivative along the
i
-th tangential direction
\tau_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,d-1
.
The operator
I-\Delta_{\Gamma}
has a compact inverse.

Let us introduce the trilinear form
b
defined by

b:V\times V_{1}\times V_{1}\ni(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi,\psi)\mapsto\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathcal{O}}\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial\phi(x)}{\partial x_{i}}\psi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\in\mathbb{R}.

Using the density of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O})
in
V_{1}
together with the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, we can prove as in

[
53
, Lemmas II.1.3 & II.1.6]
that there exists
C>0
depending only on
\mathcal{O}
s.t.

\displaystyle|b(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi,\psi)\rvert\leq C\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathcal{O})}\|\phi\|_{L^{4}(\mathcal{O})}\lvert\nabla\psi\rvert\leq C\lvert\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\rvert^{\frac{1}{d}}\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{V}^{1-\frac{1}{d}}|\phi\rvert^{\frac{1}{d}}\|\phi\|_{V_{1}}^{1-\frac{1}{d}}\|\psi\|_{V_{1}},\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\psi,\;\phi\in V_{1}.

Moreover,

b(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi,\phi)=0,\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\phi\in V_{1}.

(2.7)

The previous inequality implies the following fact. There exists a continuous bilinear form

B_{1}:V\times V_{1}\to V_{1}^{\prime}

such that

\mathchoice{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-56.29979pt\hskip 56.29979pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-56.29979ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 56.29979ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-56.29979pt\hskip 56.29979pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-56.29979ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 56.29979ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.7086pt\hskip 43.7086pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-43.7086ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 43.7086ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.80994pt\hskip 38.80994pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-38.80994ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 38.80994ptV_{1}}}}=b(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi,\psi),\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\psi,\;\phi\in V_{1}.

(2.8)

In particular,
B_{1}
satisfies

\mathchoice{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-56.29979pt\hskip 56.29979pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-56.29979ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 56.29979ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-56.29979pt\hskip 56.29979pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-56.29979ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 56.29979ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.7086pt\hskip 43.7086pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-43.7086ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 43.7086ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.80994pt\hskip 38.80994pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-38.80994ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 38.80994ptV_{1}}}}=-b(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\psi,\phi),\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\psi,\;\phi\in V_{1}

(2.9)

and

\|B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi)\|_{V_{1}^{\prime}}\leq C\lvert\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\rvert^{\frac{1}{d}}\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{V}^{1-\frac{1}{d}}|\phi\rvert^{\frac{1}{d}}\|\phi\|_{V_{1}}^{1-\frac{1}{d}},\;\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\phi\in V_{1}.

(2.10)

Subsequently, if
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V
and
\phi\in V_{1}
, we have the following equality, which is to be understood in the weak sense:

\diver(\phi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})=\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla\phi+\phi\underbrace{\diver\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}_{=0}=\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla\phi.

Hence, for any test function
\psi\in V_{1}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}}\diver(\phi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{O}}[\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla\phi]\psi\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{\mathcal{O}}[\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla\psi]\phi\,\mathrm{d}x=-b(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\psi,\phi).

The following equality holds in the weak sense:

\diver_{\Gamma}(\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})\coloneq\nabla_{\Gamma}(\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})=\varphi\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}.

Thus, in particular, for all
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\varphi,\,\psi\in V_{\Gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\diver_{\Gamma}(\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}})\psi\,\mathrm{d}S=\int_{\Gamma}\varphi\psi\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\,\mathrm{d}S+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}S

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\varphi\psi\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\,\mathrm{d}S-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi\psi\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\,\mathrm{d}S-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}S=-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}S.

From now on, we introduce a trilinear form
\tilde{b}
defined by

\tilde{b}:V\times V_{\Gamma}\times V_{\Gamma}\ni(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi,\psi)\mapsto-\int_{\Gamma}\varphi\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}S\in\mathbb{R}.

\tilde{b}
is well defined, since
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Furthermore, the map
\tilde{b}
enjoys the following properties.

Lemma 2.3
.

Assume
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V
,
\varphi,\,\psi\in V_{\Gamma}
. The map
\tilde{b}
is trilinear continuous and its satisfies

|\tilde{b}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi,\psi)\rvert\leq C\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{V}\|\varphi\|_{V_{\Gamma}}\|\psi\|_{V_{\Gamma}}.

(2.11)

Moreover, there exists a bilinear form
\tilde{B}:V\times V_{\Gamma}\to V_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
such that

\mathchoice{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\tilde{B}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-49.86575pt\hskip 49.86575pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-49.86575ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 49.86575ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\tilde{B}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-49.86575pt\hskip 49.86575pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-49.86575ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 49.86575ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\tilde{B}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-39.42976pt\hskip 39.42976pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-39.42976ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 39.42976ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\tilde{B}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi),\psi\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-35.42616pt\hskip 35.42616pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-35.42616ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 35.42616ptV_{\Gamma}}}}=\tilde{b}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi,\psi),\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\;\varphi,\;\psi\in V_{\Gamma},

and there exists a positive constant
C=C(\mathcal{O},\Gamma)
such that

\|\tilde{B}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi)\|_{V_{\Gamma}^{\prime}}\leq C\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{V}\|\varphi\|_{V_{\Gamma}},\;\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V,\;\varphi\in V_{\Gamma}.

(2.12)

Proof.

Let
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V
and
\varphi,\;\psi\in V_{\Gamma}
be arbitrary. Using the Hölder inequality together with the Trace embedding theorem, see, for instance, Adams
[
6
, Theorem 5.36, Section 5]
, we infer

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{b}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi,\psi)\rvert\leq\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\Gamma)}\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma)}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C(\Gamma,\mathcal{O})\|\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\|_{V}\|\varphi\|_{V_{\Gamma}}\|\psi\|_{V_{\Gamma}}.

Hence, for an arbitrary but fixed
\psi\in V_{\Gamma}
, the mapping
\tilde{b}(\cdot,\cdot,\psi)
defined on
V\times V_{\Gamma}
with values in
\mathbb{R}
is bilinear and continuous, which implies the existence of a continuous bilinear form
\tilde{B}:V\times V_{\Gamma}\to V_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
satisfying the assertions in the lemma.
∎

To conclude this section, we introduce two continuous mappings that are also necessary for the analysis of the stochastic Brinkman system (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
).

For fixed
\phi\in V_{1},\;\varphi\in V_{\Gamma}
, we introduce the following linear maps
A_{\phi}:V_{1}\to V_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}:V_{\Gamma}\to V_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
whose actions are given by

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-44.03986pt\hskip 44.03986pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-44.03986ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 44.03986ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-44.03986pt\hskip 44.03986pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-44.03986ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 44.03986ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.12753pt\hskip 36.12753pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-36.12753ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 36.12753ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-33.13686pt\hskip 33.13686pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-33.13686ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 33.13686ptV_{1}}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{O}}M_{\mathcal{O}}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\nabla v\,\mathrm{d}x,\;\;\mu,\,v\in V_{1}=H^{1}(\mathcal{O}),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\rho\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.12437pt\hskip 43.12437pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-43.12437ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 43.12437ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\rho\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.12437pt\hskip 43.12437pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-43.12437ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 43.12437ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\rho\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-35.24286pt\hskip 35.24286pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-35.24286ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 35.24286ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\rho\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.21729pt\hskip 32.21729pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-32.21729ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 32.21729ptV_{\Gamma}}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}M_{\Gamma}(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\rho\,\mathrm{d}S,\;\;\rho,\,\theta\in V_{\Gamma}=H^{1}(\Gamma),

and by assumption
(H3)
below, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),\mu\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-44.85933pt\hskip 44.85933pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-44.85933ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 44.85933ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 10.3473pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),\mu\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-44.85933pt\hskip 44.85933pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-10.3473pt\hskip-44.85933ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 44.85933ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),\mu\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.73866pt\hskip 36.73866pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-36.73866ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 36.73866ptV_{1}}}}{{\hskip 9.4307pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle A_{\phi}(\mu),\mu\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-33.59521pt\hskip 33.59521pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-9.4307pt\hskip-33.59521ptV_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 33.59521ptV_{1}}}}

\displaystyle\geq M_{0}\lvert\nabla\mu\rvert^{2},\quad\mu\in V_{1},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\theta\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-42.64867pt\hskip 42.64867pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-42.64867ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 42.64867ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 11.0973pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\theta\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-42.64867pt\hskip 42.64867pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-11.0973pt\hskip-42.64867ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 42.64867ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\theta\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.90288pt\hskip 34.90288pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-34.90288ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 34.90288ptV_{\Gamma}}}}{{\hskip 10.1807pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta),\theta\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.97249pt\hskip 31.97249pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-10.1807pt\hskip-31.97249ptV_{\Gamma}^{\prime}\hskip 31.97249ptV_{\Gamma}}}}

\displaystyle\geq N_{0}\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\rvert^{2},\;\;\theta\in V_{\Gamma}.

Once more, thanks to the assumption
(H3)
and Hölder inequality, we can easily check that
A_{\phi}:V_{1}\to V_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}:V_{\Gamma}\to V_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
are globally Lipschitz continuous, i.e. if
\mu,\;\tilde{\mu}\in V_{1}
and
\theta,\;\tilde{\theta}\in V_{\Gamma}
,

\displaystyle\|A_{\phi}(\mu)-A_{\phi}(\tilde{\mu})\|_{V_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{M}_{0}\|\mu-\tilde{\mu}\|_{V_{1}},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta)-\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\tilde{\theta})\|_{V_{\Gamma}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{N}_{0}\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|_{V_{\Gamma}}.

3.
The Stochastic Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman Equations and main result

3.1.
Stochastic preliminaries

We explicitly define the force injecting energy into the system we will analyze. Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F}=\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space,
with filtration
\mathbb{F}=\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfying the so called usual assumptions, namely it is complete, right-continuous, and
\mathcal{F}_{0}
contains all null sets, i.e.
\mathbb{P}
-negligible subset of
\mathcal{F}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

Let
(U,(\cdot,\cdot)_{U})
and
(U_{\Gamma},(\cdot,\cdot)_{U_{\Gamma}})
be two separable Hilbert spaces. For convenience, we fix once and for all two complete standard orthonormal systems
\{e_{1,k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
on
U
and
\{e_{2,k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
on
U_{\Gamma}
, and we denote by
\mathbb{E}
the mathematical expectation w.r.t. the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
.
We assume that
W\coloneq(W^{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
, resp.
W_{\Gamma}\coloneq(W_{\Gamma}^{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
, is a
U
-cylindrical Wiener process, resp.
U_{\Gamma}
-cylindrical Wiener process on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
.
This implies that there exists a sequence
W^{k}
(resp.
W_{\Gamma}^{k}
) of i.i.d. standard real Wiener processes such that

W(t,\cdot)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}W^{k}(t,\cdot)e_{1,k}(\cdot),\;\;W_{\Gamma}(t,\cdot)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}W_{\Gamma}^{k}(t,\cdot)e_{2,k}(\cdot),

(3.1)

where the series are convergent almost surely in appropriate Hilbert or Banach spaces.

It is well known that the series in (
3.1
) do not converge in
U
, resp. in
U_{\Gamma}
, unless
U
, resp.
U_{\Gamma}
, is finite dimensional. Hence,
W
, resp.
W_{\Gamma}
, is not rigorously defined as a
U
-valued continuous process, resp.
U_{\Gamma}
-valued continuous process. Consequently, we will occasionally make use of a larger space
U_{0}\supset U
, resp.
U_{\Gamma}^{0}\supset U_{\Gamma}
, such that the embeddings of
U\hookrightarrow U_{0}
, resp.
U_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow U_{\Gamma}^{0}
, are Hilbert-Schmidt.

Example 3.1
.

An example of such space is the following

U_{0}\coloneq\left\{\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}e_{1,k}:\;\|\psi\|_{U_{0}}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}^{2}/k^{2}<\infty\right\},

where
\|u\|_{U_{0}}\coloneq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}|(u,e_{1,k})_{U}\rvert^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
for all
u\in U
.

Moreover, by standard martingale arguments it follows that the processes
W(t)
, resp.
W_{\Gamma}(t)
has a continuous
U_{0}
, resp.
U_{0}^{\Gamma}
-valued modification.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\tilde{K}
be a separable Hilbert space,
\tilde{W}
be a
\tilde{K}
-cylindrical Wiener process on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and
\tilde{H}
be a Hilbert space. We denote by
\mathcal{M}^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathscr{T}_{2}(\tilde{K},\tilde{H}))
the space of all equivalence classes of
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable processes
\phi:\Omega\times[0,T]\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(\tilde{K},\tilde{H})
satisfying

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\phi(t)\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\tilde{K},\tilde{H})}^{2}\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

For any
\phi\in\mathcal{M}^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathscr{T}_{2}(\tilde{K},\tilde{H}))
, we have, by the theory of stochastic integration on infinite dimensional Hilbert space, cf.
[
42
, Chapter 5, Section 26]
or
[
19
, Chapter 4]
, that the process
M
defined by

M(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{W}(s),\;\;t\in[0,T],

is a
\tilde{H}
-valued martingale.

Moreover, the following Itô isometry holds,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\phi(s)\mathrm{d}\tilde{W}(s)\right\|_{\tilde{H}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\|\phi(s)\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\tilde{K},\tilde{H})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

(3.2)

and by the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy (BDG) inequality,

\mathbb{E}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\|\int_{0}^{s}\phi(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tilde{W}(\tau)\right\|_{\tilde{H}}^{p}\leq C(p)\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\|\phi(s)\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\tilde{K},\tilde{H})}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}},\;p\in[1,\infty),\,t\in[0,T].

(3.3)

The above Definition
3.2
can be applied to the case of
\Gamma
.
Thus, the process defines by

M_{\Gamma}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{\Gamma}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{\Gamma}(s),\;\phi_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T];\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},\tilde{H})),

enjoys the same properties as for the stochastic process
M=(M(t):t\in[0,T])
.

Below, we denote by
\mathcal{M}^{d\times d}(\mathbb{R})
the vector space of all real
d\times d
-matrices and recall an important result on stochastic ODEs in finite dimension Hilbert spaces.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let the maps
\boldsymbol{\sigma}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega\to\mathcal{M}^{d\times d}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mbox{\boldmath{$b$}}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be both continuous in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\omega\in\Omega
, and be progressively measurable on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
. Assume that the following conditions are satisfied for all
R>0
:

\int\limits_{0}^{T}\sup\limits_{\lvert x\rvert\leq R}(\lvert\mbox{\boldmath{$b$}}(t,x)\rvert+\lvert\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t,x)\rvert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
;

2(x-y)\cdot(\mbox{\boldmath{$b$}}(t,x)-\mbox{\boldmath{$b$}}(t,y))+\lvert\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t,x)-\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t,y)\rvert^{2})\leq K_{t}(R),\;\;t\in[0,T],\,x,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\lvert x\rvert,\,\lvert y\rvert\leq R
;

2x\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$b$}}(t,x)+\lvert\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t,x)\rvert^{2})\leq K_{1}(t)(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}),\;\;t\in[0,T],\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\lvert x\rvert\leq R
;
where
K_{t}(R)
is an
\mathbb{R}^{+}\text{-}
valued
(\mathcal{F}_{t})\text{-}
adapted process satisfying

\alpha_{s}(R)\coloneq\int_{0}^{s}K_{t}(R)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\quad s\in(0,T].

Then, for any
\mathcal{F}_{0}\text{-}
measurable map
\boldsymbol{X}_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists a unique global solution to the stochastic differential equation

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{X}(t)=\mbox{\boldmath{$b$}}(t,\boldsymbol{X}(t))\mathrm{d}t+\boldsymbol{\sigma}(t,\boldsymbol{X}(t))\,\mathrm{d}W(t).

Proof.

We refer to
[
45
, Theorem 3.1.1]
.
∎

Before formulating our main assumptions, we recall the following definitions of strong and Borel measurability of an
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
-valued operator that we need in this work. Here,
\mathbb{K}
and
\mathbb{X}
are two given separable Hilbert spaces.

Let us fix a measurable space
(X,\mathscr{X})
and
Y
a Banach space. Let us denote the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
Y
by
\mathcal{B}(Y)
.

Definition 3.4
.

(
[
35
, Sec 1.1, p. 2]
)
Assume that
\mathscr{Y}
is a
\sigma
-field of subsets of
Y
. A function
f:X\to Y
is
\mathscr{X}/\mathscr{Y}
measurable iff the pre-image
f^{-1}(A)\in\mathscr{X}
for any set
A\in\mathscr{Y}
.

In particular, a function
f:X\to Y
is
\mathscr{X}
-Borel iff it is
\mathscr{X}/\mathcal{B}(Y)
measurable.

Definition 3.5
.

A function
f:X\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{X})
is called strongly
\mathscr{X}
-Borel measurable if for every
k\in\mathbb{K}
, the
\mathbb{X}
-valued functions

f(\cdot)k:X\ni\psi\mapsto f(\psi)k\in\mathbb{X}
is
\mathscr{X}
-Borel according to Definition
3.4
.

To prove the existence of weak solutions in the case of nondegenerate viscosity
\nu
, nondenegerate permeability
\lambda
, nondegenerate friction
\gamma
, and regular potentials
F
and
G
, we make the following assumptions.

Assumption 3.6
.

(H1)

The set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d=2,3
, is bounded and of class
C^{2}
.

(H2)

The maps

F_{1}:V_{1}\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(U,V_{1})\mbox{ and }F_{2}:V_{\Gamma}\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},V_{\Gamma})

are measurable in the sense of Definition
3.5
.

Moreover, we assume that

\displaystyle F_{1}:V_{1}\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(U,L^{2}(\mathcal{O}))\mbox{ and }F_{2}:V_{\Gamma}\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},L^{2}(\Gamma))

are Lipschitz-continuous, while

F_{1}:V_{1}\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(U,V_{1})\mbox{ and }F_{2}:V_{\Gamma}\to\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},V_{\Gamma})

have linear growth. More precisely, there exist positive constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\|F_{1}(\phi_{1})-F_{1}(\phi_{2})\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(U,L^{2}(\mathcal{O})))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\lvert\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\rvert^{2},\;\;

\displaystyle\phi_{1},\,\phi_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O}),

(3.4)

\displaystyle\|F_{1}(\phi)\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(U,V_{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(1+\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert^{2}),\;\;

\displaystyle\phi\in V_{1},

and there exist positive constants
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
such that

\displaystyle\|F_{2}(\varphi_{1})-F_{2}(\varphi_{2})\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},L^{2}(\Gamma)))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\lvert\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\rvert_{\Gamma}^{2},\;\;

\displaystyle\varphi_{1},\,\varphi_{2}\in L^{2}(\Gamma),

(3.5)

\displaystyle\|F_{2}(\varphi)\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},V_{\Gamma})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}(1+\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\rvert_{\Gamma}^{2}),\;\;

\displaystyle\varphi\in V_{\Gamma}.

Furthermore, there exist positive constants
\tilde{C}_{1}
and
\tilde{C}_{2}
such that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|F_{1}(\phi)e_{1,k}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{T})}^{2}\leq\tilde{C}_{1},\mbox{ for every $\phi\in L^{2}(Q_{T})$ }

(3.6)

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|F_{2}(\varphi)e_{2,k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{T})}^{2}\leq\tilde{C}_{2},\;\;\mbox{ for every $\varphi\in L^{2}(\Sigma_{T})$}.

(3.7)

Example 3.7
.

Let
(\sigma_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(\tilde{\sigma}_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a sequence of functions such that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|\sigma_{k}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\infty\mbox{ and }\sum_{k}^{\infty}\|\tilde{\sigma}_{k}\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\infty.

Define

\displaystyle F_{1}(\phi)e_{1,k}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}\circ\phi,\;\;k\in\{1,2,\ldots\},\;\;\phi\in V_{1}

and

F_{2}(\varphi)e_{2,k}=\tilde{\sigma}_{k}\circ\varphi,\;\;k\in\{1,2,\ldots\},\;\;\varphi\in V_{\Gamma}.

(H3)

The maps
M_{\mathcal{O}}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
M_{\Gamma}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous, bounded and uniformly positive, i.e. there exist positive constants

M_{0},\,\bar{M}_{0},\,N_{0}
and
\bar{N}_{0}
such that

M_{0}\leq M_{\mathcal{O}}(s)\leq\bar{M}_{0},\quad N_{0}\leq M_{\Gamma}(s)\leq\bar{N}_{0},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

(H4)

Function
\nu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathcal{O})
is uniformly positive and bounded, i.e. there exist positive constants
\nu_{0}
and
\bar{\nu}_{0}
such that

\nu_{0}\leq\nu(s)\leq\bar{\nu}_{0},\;\;s\in\mathbb{R}.

(H5)

Function
\lambda\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathcal{O})
is positive and bounded, i.e. there exists a positive constant
\lambda_{0}
such that

0<\lambda(s)\leq\lambda_{0},\;\;s\in\mathbb{R}.

(H6)

Function
\gamma\in C(\mathcal{O})
is uniformly positive and bounded, i.e. there exist positive constants
\gamma_{0}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
such that

\gamma_{0}\leq\gamma(s)\leq\bar{\gamma}_{0},\;\;s\in\mathbb{R}.

(H7)

Functions
F\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
G\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
are nonnegative,
F^{\prime}(0)=0
,
G^{\prime}(0)=0
, and there exist
C_{F}>0
,
\tilde{c}_{F}>0
,
C_{G}>0
, and
\tilde{c}_{G}>0
such that

\displaystyle\lvert F^{\prime}(r)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{F}(1+F(r)),\;\;

\displaystyle r\in\mathbb{R},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\lvert F^{\prime\prime}(r)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{F}(1+F(r)),\;\;

\displaystyle r\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(r)

\displaystyle\geq-\tilde{c}_{F},\;\;

\displaystyle r\in\mathbb{R},

and

\displaystyle\lvert G^{\prime}(r)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{G}(1+G(r)),\;\;

\displaystyle r\in\mathbb{R},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\lvert G^{\prime\prime}(r)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{G}(1+G(r)),\;\;

\displaystyle r\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle G^{\prime\prime}(r)

\displaystyle\geq-\tilde{c}_{G},\;\;

\displaystyle r\in\mathbb{R}.

Remark 3.8
.

(i)
As an example of regular potential satisfying conditions (
3.8
) or (
3.9
), we can consider the following polynomial

F_{\text{pol}}(s)=\frac{\alpha}{4}(s^{2}-\beta^{2})^{2},\;\;s\in\mathbb{R},

(3.10)

where
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

F_{\text{pol}}
can be seen as an approximation of the Flory-Huggins logarithmic potential introduced in
[
15
]
:

F_{\log}(s)=\frac{\Theta}{2}[(1+s)\log(1+s)-(1-s)\log(1-s)]-\frac{\Theta_{c}}{2}s^{2},\;\;s\in(-1,1),

(3.11)

where
\Theta
and
\Theta_{c}
are positive constants denoting, respectively, the absolute temperature and the critical temperature of the mixture. In particular,
\Theta
and
\Theta_{c}
satisfy the physical relation
0<\Theta<\Theta_{c}
, where the phase separation occurs.
In general,
F_{\log}
is referred to as a singular potential since its derivative
F_{\log}^{\prime}
blows up at the pure phases
\pm 1
, and the parameter
\alpha
in (
3.10
) is related to
\Theta
and
\Theta_{c}
, whereas
\pm\beta
are the two minima of
F_{\log}
.

(iii)
Problem (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
) can also be analyzed in the case of singular potential, but due to the size of the paper, we have not consider this issue here and we postpone that question to a subsequent paper. Let us point out that the potential
F_{\log}
ensures the existence of physical solutions, i.e. solutions such that
\phi,\,\varphi\in[-1,1]
, and this is most coherent with the physical derivation of the Cahn-Hilliard model itself in relation to its thermodynamical consistency.

3.2.
Abstract formulation

We set
\mathcal{U}=U\times U_{\Gamma}
.

Then, given
\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}\in V
, define the following maps,

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{V}\ni\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}=(\phi,\varphi)\mapsto\Bigl(-B_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\phi)-A_{\phi}(\mu),-\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}(\theta)-\tilde{B}(\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}},\varphi)\Bigr)\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{V}\ni\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}=(\phi,\varphi)\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(F_{1}(\phi),F_{2}(\varphi))\in\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{H}),

where, given
\phi\in H^{2}(\mathcal{O})
,
\mu
is defined by

\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta\phi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\phi)\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O}),

(3.12)

while, given
\varphi\in D(\Delta_{\Gamma})=H^{2}(\Gamma)
,
\theta
is defined by

\theta=-\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}G^{\prime}(\varphi)+\varepsilon\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\phi.

(3.13)

Therefore, we rewrite the equations (
1.1c
) and (
1.2a
) in the following ”compact” form

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}(t)=\boldsymbol{b}(\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}(t))\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{W}(t),\\
\mu(t)=-\varepsilon\Delta\phi(t)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\phi(t)),\;\;t\in[0,T],\\
\theta(t)=-\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi(t)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}G^{\prime}(\varphi(t))+\varepsilon\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\phi(t),\;\;t\in[0,T].\end{cases}

(3.14)

Now we define what we mean by weak martingale solutions to the problem (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
).

Definition 3.9
.

Assume
K>0
and let
(\phi_{0},\varphi_{0}):\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{V}
be a non-random initial data. By a weak martingale solution to (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
), we mean a system consisting of a complete probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
, with the filtration
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}=(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfying the usual conditions, and a process

(\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}},\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\mathcal{W}})=\left(\left(\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}(t),\tilde{\phi}(t),\tilde{\varphi}(t),\tilde{\mu}(t),\tilde{\theta}(t),\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(t)\right):t\in[0,T]\right)

(3.15)

such that

\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is a cylindrical Wiener process in a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{U}
defined on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}})
.

\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}:\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to V
and
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta}):\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{V}

are
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
progressively measurable processes with
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.e. paths

\displaystyle\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\in L^{2}(0,T;V),\quad\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\lvert_{\Gamma}\,\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\Gamma)),

(3.16)

\displaystyle(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta})\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),

\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}\coloneq(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\varphi}):\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{V}
is a
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
-progressively measurable processes with
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.e. paths

\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}\in C_{w}([0,T];\mathbb{V})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),

(3.17)

such that for any
\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\in V
,
(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\in\mathbb{V}
, and
(\psi,\psi\lvert_{\Gamma})\in\mathbb{V}
, and every
t\in[0,T]
, the following equalities hold
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle 2\int_{Q_{t}}\nu(\tilde{\phi})D\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}:\nabla\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{Q_{t}}\lambda(\tilde{\phi})\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\gamma(\tilde{\varphi})\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.18)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\tilde{\varphi}\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{\theta}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{Q_{t}}\tilde{\phi}\nabla\tilde{\mu}\cdot\mbox{\boldmath{$v$}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(t),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.41676pt\hskip 60.41676pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-60.41676pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 60.41676pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(t),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.41676pt\hskip 60.41676pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-60.41676pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 60.41676pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(t),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-49.67387pt\hskip 49.67387pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-49.67387pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 49.67387pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(t),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-46.36032pt\hskip 46.36032pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-46.36032pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 46.36032pt\mathbb{V}}}}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\boldsymbol{b}(\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(s)),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-74.47925pt\hskip 74.47925pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-74.47925pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 74.47925pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 74.47925pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-74.47925pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 74.47925pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\boldsymbol{b}(\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(s)),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.32318pt\hskip 60.32318pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-60.32318pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 60.32318pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\boldsymbol{b}(\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(s)),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-54.91359pt\hskip 54.91359pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-54.91359pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 54.91359pt\mathbb{V}}}}\mathrm{d}s

(3.19)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle(\phi_{0},\varphi_{0}),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-77.16676pt\hskip 77.16676pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-77.16676pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 77.16676pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 8.6077pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle(\phi_{0},\varphi_{0}),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-77.16676pt\hskip 77.16676pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.6077pt\hskip-77.16676pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 77.16676pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle(\phi_{0},\varphi_{0}),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.2351pt\hskip 61.2351pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-61.2351pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 61.2351pt\mathbb{V}}}}{{\hskip 7.60774pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle(\phi_{0},\varphi_{0}),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-56.6047pt\hskip 56.6047pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.60774pt\hskip-56.6047pt\mathbb{V}^{\prime}\hskip 56.6047pt\mathbb{V}}}}+\left(\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\sigma}(\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$X$}}}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(s),(\upsilon,\upsilon\lvert_{\Gamma})\right)_{\mathbb{H}},

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{t}}\tilde{\mu}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\tilde{\theta}\psi\lvert_{\Gamma}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{Q_{t}}\nabla\tilde{\phi}\cdot\nabla\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{Q_{t}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\tilde{\phi})\psi\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.20)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{\varphi}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\lvert_{\Gamma}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}G^{\prime}(\tilde{\varphi})\psi\lvert_{\Gamma}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+(1/K)\int_{\Sigma_{t}}(\tilde{\varphi}-\tilde{\phi})(\varepsilon\psi\lvert_{\Gamma}-\varepsilon\psi)\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s.

Definition 3.10
.

We introduce the Lyapunov functional

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{tot}:\mathbb{V}\ni(\phi,\varphi)\mapsto

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\lvert\nabla\phi\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}\lvert\phi\rvert^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\Gamma}\left[\frac{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}{2}\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}G(\varphi)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2K}(\varphi-\phi)^{2}\right]\,\mathrm{d}S\in[0,\infty),

(3.21)

where the integral
\int_{\Gamma}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}S
is the integral with respect to the ”riemannian volume” measure.

We are ready to state our main result.

Theorem 3.11
.

Assume the assumptions
(H1)
-
(H7)
hold.
Moreover, suppose that there exist
c_{F^{\prime}}>0
and
c_{G^{\prime}}>0
such that for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\lvert F^{\prime}(r)\rvert\leq c_{F^{\prime}}(1+\lvert r\rvert^{p-1}),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\lvert G^{\prime}(r)\rvert\leq c_{G^{\prime}}(1+\lvert r\rvert^{q-1}),

where

p\in\begin{cases}[2,\infty)&\mbox{ if }d=2,\\
[2,6]&\mbox{ if }d=3,\end{cases}\mbox{ and }q\in[2,\infty),

and there exist
c_{F^{\prime\prime}}>0
and
c_{G^{\prime\prime}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\lvert F^{\prime\prime}(r)\rvert\leq c_{F^{\prime\prime}}(1+\lvert r\rvert^{p-2}),\;\;\forall r\in\mathbb{R},

(3.23)

\displaystyle\lvert G^{\prime\prime}(r)\rvert\leq c_{G^{\prime\prime}}(1+\lvert r\rvert^{q-2}),\;\;\forall r\in\mathbb{R},

where

p\in\begin{cases}[2,\infty)&\mbox{ if }d=2,\\
(0,4]&\mbox{ if }d=3,\end{cases}\mbox{ and }q\in[2,\infty).

Then, there exists a weak martingale solution

\left(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}},\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta},\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\right)

to system (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
) in the sense of Definition
3.9
. Moreover, the process
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta})
satisfies

(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta})\in L^{4}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),\quad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\text{-a.s.,}

(3.24)

and we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{tot}(\tilde{\phi}(s),\tilde{\varphi}(s))]+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{Q_{t}}[2\nu(\tilde{\phi})\lvert D\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\tilde{\phi})\lvert\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\rvert^{2}]\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\gamma(\tilde{\varphi})\lvert\tilde{\mbox{\boldmath{$u$}}}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{Q_{t}}M_{\mathcal{O}}(\tilde{\phi})\lvert\nabla\tilde{\mu}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{\Sigma_{t}}M_{\Gamma}(\tilde{\varphi})\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{\theta}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{tot}(\phi_{0},\varphi_{0})+C\bigg[(1/K)+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\|F_{1}(\tilde{\phi})\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(U,L^{2}(\mathcal{O}))}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\bar{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\lvert\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{\varphi}\rvert_{\Gamma}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.25)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1+1/K\right)\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\lvert\nabla\tilde{\phi}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+(1/K)\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\|F_{2}(\tilde{\varphi})\|_{\mathscr{T}_{2}(U_{\Gamma},L^{2}(\Gamma))}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{O}}\lvert F^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{\phi})\rvert\lvert F_{1}(\tilde{\phi})e_{1,k}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\Gamma}\lvert G^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{\varphi})\rvert\lvert F_{2}(\tilde{\varphi})e_{2,k}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg],\;\;t\in[0,T].

where the constant
C
depends on
\varepsilon,\,\Gamma,\,\varepsilon_{\Gamma},\,C_{1},\,C_{2},\,C_{\Gamma,\mathcal{O}}
, and
\bar{M}_{0}
.

If
\mathcal{O}
is of class
C^{\iota}
,
\iota\in\{2,3\}
, then

\displaystyle(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\varphi})\in L^{4}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{2}),\;\;

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-a.s.},

(3.26)

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}(\tilde{\phi}),G^{\prime}(\tilde{\varphi}))\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),\;\;

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-a.s.},

(3.27)

and the process
(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\theta})
satisfies the equations (
1.1d
) and (
1.2b
) in the strong sense, i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}=-\varepsilon\Delta\tilde{\phi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\tilde{\phi})

\displaystyle\mbox{ a.e. in }Q_{T},\;\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{ -a.s., }

(3.28)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}=-\varepsilon_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}\tilde{\varphi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\Gamma}}G^{\prime}(\tilde{\varphi})+\varepsilon\partial_{\mbox{\boldmath{$n$}}}\tilde{\phi}

\displaystyle\mbox{ a.e. on }\Sigma_{T},\;\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{ -a.s. }

(3.29)

If
\mathcal{O}
is of class
C^{3}
, then

(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\varphi})\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{3}),\;\;\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-a.s.}

(3.30)

The complete proof of Theorem
3.11
is postponed in Section
6
.

4.
Existence of global weak solution

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.11
. To be precise, we will prove the existence of a global weak martingale solution to the stochastic Cahn-Hilliard-Brinkman system, i.e. the problem (
1.1a
)-(
1.4a
). To achieve this, we introduce an approximation scheme by combining the Yosida and Galerkin approximation schemes. First, we discuss the existence of the approximation scheme. Next, we prove a priori estimates that are useful for establishing the tightness of the family of Galerkin approximations. In a subsequent step, we construct our new stochastic basis by exploiting the Skorokhod-Jakubowski Theorem in the framework of non-metric spaces, see
[
36
]
, and then pass to the limit in the approximating scheme. Finally, we derive additional a priori estimates that are useful for establishing the tightness of the family of Yosida approximation problems.

4.1.
Yosida approximation

Define the following non-decreasing and continuous map

\tilde{F}:\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto\left[F(s)+\frac{\tilde{c}_{F}}{2}s^{2}\right]\in[0,\infty).

By assumption
(H7)
, function
\tilde{F}
takes finite values at every point in its domain
D(\tilde{F})=\mathbb{R}
. In particular,
\tilde{F}
is a proper, convex, and lower semicontinuous function. Therefore, invoking the theory of maximal monotone operators, see, e.g.,

[
7
, Chapter II]
or
[
11
,
47
]
, the subgradient of
\tilde{F}
exists and we put

\mathbb{A}=\partial\tilde{F}:D(\mathbb{A})=\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}.

Let us recall the following result, which establishes the action of the subgradient operator at the regularity points, see
[
7
, Chapter II, Example 3]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume that the map
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is convex and differentiable at some
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,
\partial\psi(s)=\psi^{\prime}(s)
.

Thanks to the previous lemma and because
\tilde{F}
is of
C^{1}
-class on
\mathbb{R}
, we infer that

\mathbb{A}(s)=\tilde{F}^{\prime}(s),\;\;s\in\mathbb{R},

(4.1)

where
\tilde{F}^{\prime}(s)
refers to the standard derivative of
\tilde{F}
at
s
.

For every
\delta\in(0,1)
, we define a function
\tilde{F}_{\delta}
by

\tilde{F}_{\delta}(x)=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\frac{1}{2\delta}\lvert x-y\rvert^{2}+\tilde{F}(y)\right\},\;\;x\in\mathbb{R}

and define

\displaystyle J_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto(I+\delta\mathbb{A})^{-1}(s)\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto\frac{1}{\delta}(s-J_{\delta}(s))\in\mathbb{R},

the
1
-Lipschitz continuous resolvent operator and the Yosida approximation of
\mathbb{A}
, respectively.

By the elementary properties of the maximal monotone operators, see, for instance,
[
7
,
11
,
47
]
,
we approximate the potential
F
by a sequence of nonnegative functions
(F_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
, called the Yosida approximation of
F
:

F_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\ni s\mapsto\left[\frac{\delta}{2}\lvert\mathbb{A}_{\delta}(s)\rvert^{2}+\tilde{F}(J_{\delta}(s))-\frac{\tilde{c}_{F}}{2}s^{2}\right]\in[0,\infty).

(4.2)

Furthermore,
\tilde{F}_{\delta}
and
F_{\delta}
enjoy the following properties:

(P1)

\tilde{F}_{\delta}(s)=\frac{\delta}{2}\lvert\mathbb{A}_{\delta}(s)\rvert^{2}+\tilde{F}(J_{\delta}(s))
, for every
\delta>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(P2)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}F_{\delta}(s)=F(s)
, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(P3)

\tilde{F}(J_{\delta}(s))\leq\tilde{F}_{\delta}(s)\leq\tilde{F}(s)
, for every
\delta>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(P4)

F^{\prime}_{\delta}(r)=\mathbb{A}_{\delta}(r)-\tilde{c}_{F}r,\;r\in\mathbb{R}
, and
F^{\prime}_{\delta}
is Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}
with constant
L_{F_{\delta}^{\prime}}\coloneq\frac{1}{\delta}+\tilde{c}_{F}
,

(P5)

\lvert F^{\prime}_{\delta}(s)\rvert\nearrow\lvert F^{\prime}(s)\rvert
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
as
\delta\to 0
,

(P6)

F_{\delta}(0)=F(0)
and
F^{\prime}_{\delta}(0)=0
, for every
\delta>0
.

Remark 4.2
.

Since
\mathbb{A}_{\delta}
is differentiable on
